## Glossary

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Concept A** | The aircraft being built: a scaled quad tailsitter. About 1.47 kg, 0.90 m wing, four motors in an X, sits on its fin tips, takes off like a quadcopter, pitches 90 degrees and flies like a plane, then pitches back to land. |
| **Tailsitter** | An aircraft that takes off and lands standing on its tail. Here the whole aircraft tips forward to change between hover and wing-borne flight. |
| **Hover mode / cruise mode** | Hover: nose up, the motors hold it up like a quadcopter. Cruise: nose forward, the wing makes the lift, as in an ordinary plane. |
| **Transition** | The few seconds in which the aircraft pitches between hover and cruise. Thrust and control are tight here. |
| **Cage / hub / arms / bridge** | The printed and tube structure that holds the four motors. Arms are the carbon tubes to the motors, the hub joins them, the bridge tube carries the hub load into the wing spar. |
| **Elevon** | A wing flap that works as both elevator (pitch) and aileron (roll). Two of them, one per wing half. |
| **LE / chord** | LE is the wing's leading edge. Chord is the front-to-back width of the wing (245 mm here); positions are measured aft of the LE. |
| **CG** | Centre of gravity: the point where the aircraft balances. Measured in mm aft of the leading edge. Too far aft makes it unstable; too far forward makes it hard to pitch. |
| **NP** | Neutral point: the position of the CG at which the aircraft is exactly neutrally stable in pitch. The CG must sit ahead of it. |
| **SM / static margin** | Static margin = (NP - CG) / chord, as a percent. It measures how far ahead of the neutral point the CG sits. At least 5 % chord is required. |
| **T/W** | Thrust-to-weight ratio: total thrust of the four motors divided by the aircraft's weight. Above 1 it can lift off; the team requires 2.0 with a fresh pack and 1.6 with a worn one. |
| **Fresh / sag** | Fresh pack: fully charged, 16.8 V for four cells. Sag: the pack nearly empty (20 % left) with its voltage dropping under load (14.92 V at rest in the model); thrust is lower. |
| **Spin cap / duty** | Duty is the fraction of full throttle. The flight software never commands more than 0.90, so one motor can never draw the current of full throttle. |
| **Derate** | A knock-down multiplier (0.93) applied to the published propeller thrust table because real thrust is usually lower than a vendor table. |
| **Cant** | The sideways tilt of each motor's thrust line (17 degrees) that gives yaw authority. |
| **ESC** | Electronic speed controller: the box that drives a motor from the battery. One per motor. Rated in amps. |
| **4S, mAh, Wh** | 4S: four lithium cells in series (14.8 V nominal, 16.8 V full). mAh or Ah: charge capacity. Wh: energy (Ah x volts). |
| **MN2212, KV780, APC 9x4.5MR** | The motor (T-Motor MN2212, 780 rpm per volt) and the propeller (APC, 9 in diameter, 4.5 in pitch) used on all four positions. |
| **Gate / mass gate** | A limit a design number must pass before the team may order or fly. The mass gate is 1.47 kg: the as-built aircraft must weigh at most that. |
| **EST / ESTIMATE** | A number the team estimated rather than measured or took from a vendor page. EST inputs are the reason many claims are UNVERIFIED. |
| **UNVERIFIED / CHECKED / VERIFIED / PROPOSED** | The status words. See the status list at the top of this page. |
| **Assumption (ASM-)** | A guess the team has written down on purpose, with the reasoning, so it can be replaced by a measurement later. |
| **Margin** | How much room a result has to its limit, as a percent of the limit. Positive passes. Negative fails. |
| **MS (margin of safety)** | For structures: MS = allowable stress / design stress - 1. MS = 0 means the part is exactly at its allowable under the design load; MS = +0.5 means it can take 1.5 times that load. It is >= 0 to pass. |
| **Utilisation** | Design stress divided by allowable stress. Utilisation = 1 / (1 + MS). Utilisation 0.5 means the part uses half of what it can carry. |
| **Factor of safety** | A multiplier on the expected load so that the part still has margin if the load is bigger than predicted. Here the design load is 1.5 x limit load x 1.15 (= 1.725) for carbon and metal parts and 2.156 for printed and bonded parts. |
| **Limit load / ultimate load** | Limit load: the largest load expected in service. Ultimate: limit x the factor of safety; the part must carry ultimate without breaking. |
| **Allowable** | The stress a material or joint is allowed to carry. Many allowables here are EST until a coupon (a small test piece) is broken on a test stand. |
| **Prop strike** | The propeller hitting the ground or an object. The team treats a 30 N strike on one prop as a design load case (a damage case, factor 1.0). |
| **CFRP, PETG, XPS** | Carbon-fibre tube; PETG printed plastic (the printed parts); XPS the pink extruded foam of the wing core (FOAMULAR 150). |
| **MPa, kPa** | Units of stress. 1 MPa = 1 N per square millimetre; 1 kPa = 1/1000 of that. |
| **Fatigue** | Failure from many repeated small loads rather than one big one. Checked against the propeller's once-per-turn shake over the flight life. |
| **n, g-load, VD** | n is load factor (3.4 means 3.4 times weight). VD is the design dive speed, 24 m/s: the fastest speed the structure is designed for. |
| **Vs, CLmax** | Vs is stall speed (the lowest speed the wing can still fly). It depends on CLmax, the wing's peak lift coefficient, which is an assumption (0.75) until a flight measures it. |
| **Flutter / divergence / reversal** | Three aeroelastic failures. Flutter: the wing or control surface oscillates and grows. Divergence: the wing twists more as it gets more lift until it breaks. Reversal: the elevon works backwards. The team requires all of them to be at least 1.2 x VD = 28.8 m/s. |
| **Yaw authority** | The twisting force about the vertical axis the motors can produce for steering in hover (N m). Needs at least 0.35 N m. |
| **SITL** | Software-in-the-loop: the real autopilot software flying a simulated aircraft on a computer. Simulation results are marked CHECKED at best, never VERIFIED. |
| **BATT_LOW** | The autopilot's low-battery trigger. After it fires the aircraft must land with the energy left. |
| **FE / FEA** | Finite-element analysis: a computer model that splits a part into small pieces to find its stress. The FEA files are separate from this chain; the chain stores their results. |
| **Rotor band, 1P, 2P** | The vibration frequencies produced by the spinning propellers: 1P once per turn, 2P twice. Structures must stay 20 % away from them. |
| **Gain margin** | How much the control loop's gain could be multiplied before the loop goes unstable. At least 2 is required. |
| **BOM** | Bill of materials: the list of everything bought, with price. BOM-A.csv has 38 lines. |
| **G7** | The cost gate: total at most $1,800 and at least $100 left under $2,000 even if the unverified prices run 30 % higher. |
| **D-xxx (D-206, D-182 ...)** | Design decision numbers from the team log (redesign/params/CHANGE-LOG.md). 'D-206' is decision 206. A number in a card's source name tells you which decision changed the design. |
| **QP-n, FIXES, FIXES2** | QP-1 to QP-5 are the design review rounds (boards). FIXES and FIXES2 are the fix lists the boards produced. |
| **A-P3, A-U1, A-D2 ...** | Requirement ids in REQUIREMENTS-A.md. U = user constraint, P = performance, D = design target. Each claim lists the requirement it verifies. |
| **CL-A-..., CALC-A-..., P:..., ASM-A-..., SRC-A-..., T-A-...** | Ids used by the proof chain. CL = claim, CALC = the calculation behind it, P = a parameter (input number), ASM = assumption, SRC = source document, T = planned test. The Guide shows the readable name; the id is for searching. |
| **Part ids (WNG, FIN, CGE, PRP, PWR, GER, MEC, AVN, HWC)** | Prefixes of parts in the CAD model: WNG wing, FIN fins and ribs, CGE cage (hub, arms, tray), PRP propulsion (motors, props), PWR battery and power, GER landing gear (foot shoes), MEC mechanics (servos, linkages), AVN avionics, HWC hardware (screws, nuts). |
| **M1 ... M12** | Planned measurements in the team's test plan (for example M1 thrust stand, M5 CG on a knife edge). The 'How to check' text names the test. |
